:
This is also an immediate consequence of Part
(i)
, because if
\{\tau_{r}\}_{r=m-1}^{\infty}
is the unique sequence in
\mathbf{T}_{(m,t)}^{\theta}
, for each
r\geq m
,

\tau_{r}=\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta-,L}=\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta+,R}.

∎

We now define the events
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
of Theorem
4.7
. First, let
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}
be the events of Lemma
7.6
, and for each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, define the full probability events

(7.15)

\Omega_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}=\widetilde{\Omega}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}\cap\Omega_{2}.

By Theorem
3.5
(vii)
and Theorem
3.7
(i)
, for each
r\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s\mapsto B_{r}(s)-h_{r+1}^{\theta}(s)
is a scaled, two-sided Brownian motion with strictly negative drift. By Theorem
B.5
, for
\theta>0
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists an event
\operatorname{CM}_{r}^{(\theta)}
, of probability one, on which the set

\{t\in\mathbb{R}:B_{r}(s)-h_{r+1}^{\theta}(s)\text{ over }s\in[t,\infty)\text{ has a non-unique maximum at }s=t\}

is countably infinite. Then, for
\theta>0
, set

(7.16)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}:=\bigcap_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Q}}\Omega_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}\cap\bigcap_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{CM}_{r}^{(\theta)}.

Because
\Omega_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}=\widetilde{\Omega}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}\cap\Omega_{2}
by definition and
\Omega_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}\subseteq\Omega^{(\theta)}
by Lemma
7.6
,
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}\subseteq\Omega^{(\theta)}\cap\Omega_{2}
where
\Omega^{(\theta)}
are the events of Theorem
3.5
. Furthermore,
\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)})=1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.7
.

In this proof, since
\theta
is fixed, we drop the
\pm
distinction for
\theta
in the superscript.

Part
(i)
:
By Theorem
4.5
(ii)
, on the event
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
, there exist multiple
\theta
-directed geodesics starting from
(m,t)
if and only if
\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}<\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,R}
for some
r\geq m
. If
\omega\in\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}\subseteq\Omega_{2}
and
(m,t)
is a point whose
\theta
-directed geodesic is not unique, then at most one such semi-infinite geodesic can pass through
(m,t+\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Otherwise, two different geodesics would pass through
(m,q)
for some
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, giving two
\theta
-directed geodesics starting from
(m,q)
. This cannot hold on the event
\Omega_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}\supseteq\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
. Inductively, to get two different
\theta
-directed geodesics starting from
(m,t)
, there must be some level
r\geq m
such that all
\theta
-directed geodesics pass through
(r,t)
and then one geodesic passes through
(r+1,t)
, and the other passes through
(r,t+\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Therefore, by Definition
4.1
, on the event
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
, there exists a point
(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
whose semi-infinite geodesic in direction
\theta
is not unique if and only if there exists
r\geq m
such that, for
m\leq k\leq r-1
,
B_{k}(s)-h_{k+1}^{\theta}(s)
over
s\in[t,\infty)
has a unique maximum at
s=t
, and
B_{r}(s)-h_{r+1}^{\theta}(s)
over
s\in[t,\infty)
has a non-unique maximum at
s=t
. Therefore,
t=\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}<\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,R}
for some
r\geq m
. The countability of the sets then follows from Theorem
B.5
(iv)
because, for each
k
,
B_{k}-h_{k+1}^{\theta}
is a (scaled) Brownian motion with negative drift (Theorem
3.5
(vii)
and Theorem
3.7
(i)
).

Part
(ii)
: By Theorem
4.7
(i)
,
(m,t)\in\operatorname{NU}_{1}^{\theta}
if and only if
B_{m}(s)-h_{m+1}^{\theta}(s)
has two maximizers over
s\in[t,\infty)
–one at
s=t
and one at some
s>t
. The result then follows from Theorem
B.5

(iii)
.
∎

7.3.
The dual environment

Throughout Sections
7.3
and
7.4
,
\theta>0
is fixed
, and we work on the full probability event
\Omega^{(\theta)}
of Theorem
3.5
. By Remark
4.2
, on this event
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta}=\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta+}=\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta-}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
. Recall the dual environment
\mathbf{X}^{\theta}
of independent Brownian motions from Theorem
3.7

Lemma 7.10
.

For each
\theta>0
, there exists an event
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta,\star)}
of probability one, on which

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl[\,L_{(-n,-t_{n}),\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta})-L_{(-n,-t_{n}),\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta})\,\bigr]=\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})

for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
and for any sequence
\{t_{n}\}
satisfying
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{n}}{n}=\theta
.
To clarify,
L_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta})
is the last-passage process on the environment
\mathbf{X}^{\theta}
while
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}
is the original Busemann function of the environment
\mathbf{B}
as in Theorem
3.5
.

Proof.

Recall from Theorem
3.5
(vi)
that

h_{m-1}^{\theta}=D(h_{m}^{\theta},B_{m-1}),\qquad X_{m}^{\theta}=R(h_{m}^{\theta},B_{m-1}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{m}^{\theta}=Q(h_{m}^{\theta},B_{m-1}).

Further, recall (
3.11
), which states

(7.17)

h_{m}^{\theta}=\overleftarrow{D}(h_{m-1}^{\theta},X_{m}^{\theta}),\qquad B_{m-1}=\overleftarrow{R}(h_{m-1}^{\theta},X_{m}^{\theta}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{m}^{\theta}=\overleftarrow{Q}(h_{m-1}^{\theta},X_{m}^{\theta}).

Recall that
\widetilde{f}(t)=-f(-t)
. Apply Lemma
D.2
to deduce that

(7.18)

\widetilde{h}_{m-1}^{\theta}=\overleftarrow{D}(\widetilde{h}_{m}^{\theta},\widetilde{B}_{m-1}),\qquad\widetilde{X}_{m}^{\theta}=\overleftarrow{R}(\widetilde{h}_{m}^{\theta},\widetilde{B}_{m-1}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad-\widetilde{v}_{m}^{\theta}=\overleftarrow{Q}(\widetilde{h}_{m}^{\theta},\widetilde{B}_{m-1}).

From independence and matching marginals (Part Theorem
3.5
(vii)
and Theorem
3.7
, Parts
(i)
and
(ii)
) follows

(7.19)

(\,\widetilde{h}_{k}^{\theta},\widetilde{B}_{k-1},\widetilde{B}_{k-2},\widetilde{B}_{k-3},\dotsc)\overset{d}{=}(h_{m-1}^{\theta},X_{m}^{\theta},X_{m+1}^{\theta},X_{m+2}^{\theta},\dotsc)\qquad\forall k,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

Thus, iterating mappings (
7.18
) backward in the index
m
and mappings (
7.17
) forward in the index
m
gives this equality in distribution:

\bigl\{\widetilde{h}_{-(m-1)}^{\theta},-\widetilde{v}_{-(m-1)}^{\theta},\widetilde{X}_{-(m-1)}^{\theta},\widetilde{B}_{-m}\bigr\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\overset{d}{=}\bigl\{h_{m-1}^{\theta},v_{m}^{\theta},B_{m-1},X_{m}^{\theta}\bigr\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}

Apply Theorem
3.5
(viii)
to the environment
\mathbf{\widetilde{X}_{-}^{\theta}}=\{\widetilde{X}_{-m}^{\theta}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
in the process on the left above, to deduce that there exists a full probability event
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta,\star)}
on which,
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{(-m,-t),(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{\widetilde{X}_{-}^{\theta}})-L_{(-m,0),(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{\widetilde{X}_{-}^{\theta}})=-\widetilde{h}_{-(-m)}^{\theta}(-t)=h_{m}^{\theta}(t)\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{(-m,-t),(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{\widetilde{X}_{-}^{\theta}})-L_{(-m+1,-t),(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{\widetilde{X}_{-}^{\theta}})=-\widetilde{v}_{-(-m)}^{\theta}(-t)=v_{m}^{\theta}(t).

Adding together horizontal and vertical steps for the general case, the proof is complete by noting that, for any
m,k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{(-m,-s),(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{\widetilde{X}_{-}^{\theta}})-L_{(-k,-t),(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{\widetilde{X}_{-}^{\theta}})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{(-n,-t_{n}),(m,s)}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta})-L_{(-n,-t_{n}),(k,t)}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta})

because

\displaystyle L_{(-m,-s),(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{\widetilde{X}_{-}^{\theta}})=\sup\Bigl\{\sum_{r=-m}^{n}\widetilde{X}_{-r}^{\theta}(s_{r-1},s_{r}):-s=s_{-m-1}\leq\cdots\leq s_{n}=t_{n}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sup\Bigl\{\sum_{r=-m}^{n}X_{-r}^{\theta}(-s_{r},-s_{r-1}):-s=s_{-m-1}\leq\cdots\leq s_{n}=t_{n}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sup\Bigl\{\sum_{r=-m}^{n}X_{-r}^{\theta}(-s_{r},-s_{r-1}):-t_{n}=-s_{n}\leq-s_{n-1}\leq\cdots\leq-s_{m-1}=s\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sup\Bigl\{\sum_{r=-n}^{m}X_{r}^{\theta}(\widetilde{s}_{r-1},\widetilde{s}_{r}):-t_{n}=\widetilde{s}_{-n-1}\leq\widetilde{s}_{-n}\leq\cdots\leq\widetilde{s}_{m}=s\Bigr\}=L_{(-n,-t_{n}),(m,s)}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta}).

To get the second-to-last line above, simply set
\widetilde{s}_{k}=-s_{-k-1}
.
∎

7.4.
Dual geodesics

Recall the definition of the sets
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta\star}
from Section
4.4
. Analogous results as for the original northwest semi-infinite geodesics hold, as demonstrated by the following theorem.

Theorem 7.11
.

Fix
\theta>0
. Then, for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, every semi-infinite path in
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta,\star}
is a semi-infinite geodesic for Brownian last-passage percolation with environment
\mathbf{X}^{\theta}
. Specifically, the following hold.

(i)

On the full probability event
\Omega^{(\theta)}
, let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, and let
\Gamma^{\star}
be any semi-infinite path in
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta,\star}
. Then, for any
\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
with
\mathbf{y}^{\star},\mathbf{z}^{\star}
lying along the semi-infinite path, the energy of the portion of that path between
\mathbf{y}^{\star}
and
\mathbf{z}^{\star}
, in the environment
\mathbf{X}^{\theta}
, is

L_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta})=\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}),

and this energy is maximal among all paths between
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{z}
in the environment
\mathbf{X}^{\theta}
. To be clear,
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}
is the original Busemann function for the environment
\mathbf{B}
.

(ii)

On
\Omega^{(\theta)}
, for all
(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
and
r\leq m
,

\displaystyle\tau_{(m,t),r-1}^{\theta,R\star}=\sup\bigl\{u\leq\tau^{\theta,R\star}_{(m,t),r}:v_{r}^{\theta}(u)=0\bigr\}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\tau^{\theta,L\star}_{(m,t),r-1}=\inf\bigl\{u\leq\tau^{\theta,L\star}_{(m,t),r}:h_{r}^{\theta}(u,\tau^{\theta,L\star}_{(m,t),r})=X_{r}^{\theta}(u,\tau^{\theta,L\star}_{(m,t),r})\bigr\}

More specifically, if
u\leq\tau^{\theta,L\star}_{(m,t),r}
, then
h_{r}^{\theta}(u,\tau^{\theta,L\star}_{(m,t),r})=X_{r}^{\theta}(u,\tau^{\theta,L\star}_{(m,t),r})
if and only if
u\geq\tau^{\theta,L\star}_{(m,t),r-1}
.

(iii)

On
\Omega^{(\theta)}
, if, for some
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbf{x}^{\star}\geq\mathbf{y}^{\star}
lie along the leftmost semi-infinite geodesic in
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{z}}^{\theta,\star}
, then the portion of the path between
\mathbf{x}^{\star}
and
\mathbf{y}^{\star}
, shifted back up by
\frac{1}{2}
to lie on integer levels, is the leftmost geodesic between
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
in the environment
\mathbf{X}^{\theta}
. The analogous statement holds for the rightmost geodesics.

(iv)

The following distributional equality holds.

\displaystyle\bigl\{\bigl(\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,R},\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}\bigr):(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z},r\geq m\bigr\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\overset{d}{=}\bigl\{\bigl(-\tau_{(-m,-t),-(r+1)}^{\theta,L\star},-\tau_{(-m,-t),-(r+1)}^{\theta,R\star}\bigr):(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z},r\geq m\bigr\}

(v)

There exists an event of full probability,
\Omega^{(\theta,\star)}\subseteq\Omega^{(\theta)}
, on which, for every
(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
and every sequence
\{\tau^{\star}_{r}\}_{r\leq m}\in\mathbf{T}_{(m,t)}^{\theta,\star}
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{-n}^{\star}}{-n}=\theta.

Proof.

Parts
(i)
–
(iii)
:
On
\Omega^{(\theta)}
, let
(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, and
\{\tau_{r}^{\star}\}_{r\leq m}\in\mathbf{T}_{(m,t)}^{\theta,\star}
. By (
7.17
) and the definitions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
), for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(7.20)

\displaystyle v_{r}^{\theta}(u)

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<s\leq u}\{X_{r}^{\theta}(s,u)-h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s,u)\},

\displaystyle h_{r}^{\theta}(u,v)

\displaystyle=h_{r-1}^{\theta}(u,v)+\sup_{-\infty<s\leq v}\{X_{r}^{\theta}(s,v)-h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s,v)\}-\sup_{-\infty<s\leq u}\{X_{r}^{\theta}(s,u)-h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s,u)\}

(7.21)

\displaystyle=X_{r}^{\theta}(u,v)+\sup_{-\infty<s\leq v}\{h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s)-X_{r}^{\theta}(s)\}-\sup_{-\infty<s\leq u}\{h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s)-X_{r}^{\theta}(s)\},\text{ and}

\displaystyle B_{r-1}(u,v)

\displaystyle=X_{r}^{\theta}(u,v)+\sup_{-\infty<s\leq u}\{X_{r}^{\theta}(s,u)-h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s,u)\}-\sup_{-\infty<s\leq v}\{X_{r}^{\theta}(s,v)-h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s,v)\}

(7.22)

\displaystyle=h_{r-1}^{\theta}(u,v)+\sup_{-\infty<s\leq u}\{h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s)-X_{r}^{\theta}(s)\}-\sup_{-\infty<s\leq v}\{h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s)-X_{r}^{\theta}(s)\}.

As
\tau_{r-1}^{\star}
is a maximizer of
h_{r-1}(s)-X_{r}^{\theta}(s)
over
s\in(\infty,\tau_{r}^{\star}]
, from (
7.20
), it follows that
v_{r}^{\theta}(\tau_{r-1}^{\star})=0
for each
r\leq m
. By (
7.21
),
h_{r}^{\theta}(u,v)=X_{r}^{\theta}(u,v)
for
u,v\in[\tau_{r-1}^{\star},\tau_{r}^{\star}]
. In general,
v_{r}^{\theta}(u)\geq 0
and
h_{r}^{\theta}(u,v)\geq X_{r}^{\theta}(u,v)
for
u\leq v
. Then, Parts
(i)
-
(iii)
follow just as for the analogous statements in the proofs of Theorem
4.3
, Parts
(i)
-
(ii)
and Lemma
7.4
.

Parts
(iv)
–
(v)
:
By (
7.19
),
\{\widetilde{h}_{r-1}^{\theta},\widetilde{X}_{r}^{\theta}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
has the same distribution as
\{h_{-(r-1)}^{\theta},B_{-r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Furthermore, the leftmost (rightmost) maximizers of
h_{r-1}^{\theta}(s)-X_{r}^{\theta}(s)
for
s\in(-\infty,t]
are the negative of the rightmost (resp. leftmost) maximizers of
\widetilde{X}_{r}^{\theta}(s)-\widetilde{h}_{r-1}^{\theta}(s)
for
s\in[-t,\infty)
, establishing Part
(iv)
. Part
(v)
then follows from Theorem
4.3
(v)
.
∎

Theorem 7.12
.

On the event
\Omega^{(\theta)}
, the following hold for all
s\leq t\in\mathbb{R}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(i)

If
\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}<t
, then also
\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}<\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,L\star}
. See Figure
7.1
for clarity.

(ii)

If
t\leq\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}
, then
\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,L^{\star}}\leq s
. See Figure
7.2
for clarity.

(iii)

If
\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,L}\leq t
, then also
\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,L}\leq\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,R\star}
. See Figure
7.3
for clarity.

(iv)

If
t<\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,L}
, then
\tau_{(m,+1,t),m}^{\theta,R\star}<s
. See Figure
7.4
for clarity.

Remark 7.13
.

All the statements of Theorem
7.12
only mention the first jump time for northeast geodesics and the first time of descent for southwest geodesics. However, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
and any point
\mathbf{y}
along the rightmost (resp. leftmost) semi-infinite geodesic in
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta}
, the rightmost (resp. leftmost) semi-infinite geodesic in
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{y}}^{\theta}
agrees with the remainder of the original semi-infinite geodesic started from
\mathbf{x}
. Thus, the results of Theorem
7.12
can be extended by induction. For example, Part
(i)
implies that if a leftmost dual southwest geodesic starts strictly to the right and below a rightmost northeast geodesic, it remains to the right and below the northeast geodesic. See Figure
7.1
.

Remark 7.14
.

Theorem
7.12
is the analogue of Lemma 4.4 in
[
Sep20
]
, which states a result for exponential last-passage percolation. In words, Theorem
7.12
says that northeast Busemann geodesics do not cross dual southwest geodesics. In this sense, the theorem gives an analogue to Pimentel’s dual tree
[
Pim16
]
. In the setting of exponential LPP, in Section 5 of
[
Sep20
]
, it is shown that semi-infinite geodesics in the original environment are competition interfaces for geodesics in the dual environment with boundary conditions given by the Busemann process. This gives some intuition on why the northeast geodesics do not cross the southwest dual geodesics. Due to the general non-uniqueness of geodesics, the construction of competition interfaces is somewhat delicate for BLPP and will be studied in future work.

Proof.

Part
(i)
:
By Lemma
7.4
, if
u\geq s
, then

(7.23)

B_{m}(s,u)=h_{m}^{\theta}(s,u)\text{ if and only if }u\leq\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}.

By Theorem
7.11
(ii)
, if
u\leq t
, then

(7.24)

X_{m+1}^{\theta}(u,t)=h_{m+1}^{\theta}(u,t)\text{ if and only if }u\geq\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,L\star}.

By (
7.21
) and (
7.22
),

\displaystyle X_{m+1}^{\theta}(u,v)=h_{m+1}^{\theta}(u,v)\iff B_{m}(u,v)=h_{m}^{\theta}(u,v)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\sup_{-\infty\leq w\leq u}\{h_{m}^{\theta}(w)-X_{m+1}^{\theta}(w)\}=\sup_{-\infty\leq w\leq v}\{h_{m}^{\theta}(w)-X_{m+1}^{\theta}(w)\}.

This along with (
7.24
) implies that if
u\leq t
,

(7.25)

B_{m}^{\theta}(u,t)=h_{m}^{\theta}(u,t)\text{ if and only if }u\geq\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,L\star}.

By assumption,
\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}<t
. Equation (
7.23
) and the monotonicity of Theorem
3.5
(ii)
imply that

(7.26)

B_{m}(s,t)<h_{m}^{\theta}(s,t).

Assume, by way of contradiction, that
\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,L^{\star}}\leq\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}
. Then, there exists
u\leq\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}
with
u\geq s\vee\tau_{(m,t),m}^{\theta,L^{\star}}
. Then, for such
u
, by (
7.23
) and (
7.25
),

B_{r}(s,u)=h_{r}^{\theta}(s,u)\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{r}^{\theta}(u,t)=h_{r}^{\theta}(u,t).

Adding these two equations gives us a contradiction to (
7.26
).

Part
(ii)
: Let
t\leq\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}
, and assume by way of contradiction, that
\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,L\star}>s
. This also implies that
s<t
since
t\geq\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,L\star}
. By Equation (
7.25
),
B_{m}(s,t)<h_{m}^{\theta}(s,t)
. However, since
s<t\leq\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}
, Equation (
7.23
), implies that
B_{m}(s,t)=h_{m}^{\theta}(s,t)
, giving the desired contradiction.

Part
(iii)
:
Assume that
\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,L}\leq t
. By Theorem
7.11
(ii)
,

\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,R\star}=\sup\{u\leq t:v_{m+1}^{\theta}(u)=0\}.

By Lemma
7.4
,

v_{m+1}^{\theta}(\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,L})=0
, so the desired conclusion follows.

Part
(iv)
: Assume that
t<\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,L}
. By Lemma
7.4
,

\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,L}=\inf\{u\geq s:v_{m+1}^{\theta}(u)=0\}.

By assumption,
\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,R\star}\leq t<\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,L}
. By Theorem
7.11
(ii)
,

v_{m+1}^{\theta}(\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,R\star})=0
, so
\tau_{(m+1,t),m}^{\theta,R\star}<s
.
∎

We use these non-intersection properties to prove Theorem
4.9
.

Proof of Theorem
4.9
.

We follow a similar compactness argument as in the proof of Lemma 4.6 in
[
Sep20
]
, appropriately modified for the semi-discrete setting of BLPP. The full probability event of this theorem is
\Omega^{(\theta)}\cap\Omega^{(\theta,\star)}\cap\Omega_{2}
, where
\Omega^{(\theta,\star)}
is the event of Theorem
7.11
(v)
. On this event, let
\mathbf{x}=(m,t),\mathbf{y}=(k,t)
. Assume that
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are disjoint, where
\Gamma_{1}
denotes the path defined by the jump times
t=\tau_{(k,t),k-1}^{\theta,R}\leq\tau_{(k,t),k}^{\theta,R}\leq\cdots
, and
\Gamma_{2}
denotes the path defined by the jump times
s=\tau_{(m,s),m-1}^{\theta,R}\leq\tau_{(m,s),m}^{\theta,R}\leq\cdots
. By Theorem
4.3
(v)
, these paths satisfy

(7.27)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{(m,s),n}^{\theta,R}}{n}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{(k,t),n}^{\theta,R}}{n}=\theta,

so both the space and time coordinates of these up-right paths go to
\infty
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
\Gamma_{2}
lies above and to the left of
\Gamma_{1}
. Then, by picking
t_{0}
large enough, there exists
k_{0}^{1}
and
k_{0}^{2}
such that
(k_{0}^{1},t_{0})\in\Gamma_{1}
and
(k_{0}^{2},t_{0})\in\Gamma_{2}
. For each
t_{0}
, there are only finitely many choices of
k_{0}^{1}
and
k_{0}^{2}
, so assume that
k_{0}^{1}
is the largest such choice and
k_{0}^{2}
is the smallest such choice. Then,
k_{0}^{1}<k_{0}^{2}
by the assumption that
\Gamma_{2}
lies above and to the left of
\Gamma_{1}
. See Figure
7.5
for clarity. For
i=1,2,\ldots
, define
t_{i}=t_{0}+i
. For each time
i
, similarly define
k_{i}^{1}
and
k_{i}^{2}
so that
k_{i}^{1}<k_{i}^{2}
and
(k_{i}^{j},t_{i})
lies on
\Gamma_{j}
for
j=1,2
. Recall the notation
m^{\star}=m-\frac{1}{2}
. Then, for
i=0,1,2,\ldots
, there exists
k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k_{i}^{1}<k_{i}^{\star}<k_{i}^{2}
.
This gives us an infinite sequence
(k_{i}^{\star},t_{i})_{i\geq 0}
such that, for each
i
, the point
\bigl(k_{i}^{\star},t_{i}\bigr)
, lies between the paths
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
(see Figure
7.5
).
Starting from each of these points
\bigl(k_{i}^{\star},t_{i}\bigr)
, let
\Gamma_{i}^{\star}
be the leftmost dual geodesic path in
\mathbf{T}_{(k_{i},t_{i})}^{\theta,\star}
. Each
\Gamma_{i}^{\star}
is an infinite, down-left path satisfying the limit condition of Theorem
7.11
(v)
, so for each
j<i
, each of the paths
\Gamma_{i}^{\star}
intersects the vertical line
t=t_{j}
. Let
k_{i,i}^{\star}=k_{i}^{\star}
, and for
j<i
, let
k_{i,j}
be the maximal integer such that
\bigl(k_{i,j}^{\star},t_{j}\bigr)
lies on the path
\Gamma_{i}^{\star}
. Since
\bigl(k_{i}^{\star},t_{i}\bigr)
lies between
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
, and
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
are rightmost semi-infinite geodesics constructed from the Busemann functions, Theorem
7.12
(i)
–
(ii)
implies that for
i\geq 0
,
\Gamma_{i}^{\star}
lies strictly below and to the right of
\Gamma_{2}
, and weakly above and to the left
of
\Gamma_{1}
(see also Remark
7.13
).

Since
k_{j}^{1}<k_{i,j}^{\star}<k_{j}^{2}
for
0\leq j\leq i
, there are only finitely many values of
k_{i,j}
for each value of
j
. Then, there exists a subsequence
k_{i_{r}}
such that for some
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k_{0}^{1}<N_{0}^{\star}<k_{0}^{2}
and all
\ell
,
k_{i_{\ell},0}=N_{0}
. Take a further subsequence
k_{i_{r_{\ell}}}
such that for some
k_{1}^{1}<N_{1}^{\star}<k_{1}^{2}
and every element of this subsequence,
k_{i_{r_{\ell}},1}=N_{1}
. Continuing in this way, there exists a sequence
(N_{i},t_{i})
of elements of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, that
(N_{i}^{\star},t_{i})
lies between the paths
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
, and such that the leftmost southwest dual semi-infinite geodesic starting from
(N_{i}^{\star},t_{i})
passes through
(N_{j}^{\star},t_{j})
for
0\leq j\leq i
. Since we chose the leftmost paths in
\mathbf{T}_{(N_{i},t_{i})}^{\theta,\star}
at each step, the paths are consistent at all space-time points. Hence, this construction gives a bi-infinite path such that, for any point along the path, the part of the path to the southwest of that point is a semi-infinite southwest dual geodesic. To get the last part of the theorem about asymptotic direction of the paths, we already showed the direction to the southwest. The direction to the northeast follows by (
7.27
) because the path lies between
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
.

The proof for the disjoint left geodesics is analogous, replacing the use of leftmost southwest dual geodesics with rightmost southwest geodesics, and now, the bi-infinite path lies weakly below and to the right of
\Gamma_{2}
and strictly above and to the left of
\Gamma_{1}
.
∎

Constructing a backwards semi-infinite path from each discrete time point. The upper red/thick path is

.

7.5.
Proof of Lemma
3.3
, Theorem
3.1
, and Parts
(iii)
(iii)(c)
and
(iv)
(iv)(b)
of Theorem
4.3

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.

We follow the procedure of the proof of Theorem 4.12 in the arXiv version of
[
Sep18
]
. We take
(m,t)=\mathbf{0}
, and the general case follows analogously. We simplify further: for any sequence
\{t_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{n}}{n}=\theta\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{-n}}{-n}=\eta,

set
\widetilde{t}_{k}=-t_{-(k+1)}
. Then,
\{\widetilde{t}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence satisfying

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\widetilde{t}_{n}}{n}=\eta\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{-n}}{-n}=\theta.

Furthermore, a geodesic path between
(-n,t_{-n})
and
(n,t_{n})
for the original field of Brownian motions
\mathbf{B}=\{B_{r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
, when reflected through the origin, becomes a geodesic path between
(-n,\widetilde{t}_{-n})
and
(n,\widetilde{t}_{n})
for the field of Brownian motions
\{\widetilde{B}_{-r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Then, without loss of generality, assume that

(7.28)

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathcal{B}^{\eta}((1,-1),\mathbf{0})\bigr]\geq\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathcal{B}^{\theta}((1,-1),\mathbf{0})\bigr].

If a geodesic between
(-n,t_{-n})
and
(n,t_{n})
passes through
\mathbf{0}
, then the geodesic cannot pass through any point above and to the left of
\mathbf{0}
. Then, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}_{<0}
,

L_{(-n,t_{-n}),\mathbf{0}}+L_{\mathbf{0},(n,t_{n})}\geq L_{(-n,t_{-n}),\mathbf{x}}+L_{\mathbf{x},(n,t_{n})}

Specifically, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,

L_{(-n,t_{-n}),\mathbf{0}}-L_{(-n,t_{-n}),(k,-k)}\geq L_{(k,-k),(n,t_{n})}-L_{\mathbf{0},(n,t_{n})}.

For fixed
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\eta}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
denote the almost sure limit

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{(-n,t_{-n}),\mathbf{x}}-L_{(-n,-t_{-n}),\mathbf{y}}.

By Theorem
3.7
(ii)
and Lemma
7.10
, the limit exists almost surely, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\theta}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{B}^{\theta}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})
. Let
\Omega_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the full probability event on which, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and for every sequence
\{t_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{n}}{n}=\theta\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{-n}}{-n}=\eta,

we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{(-n,t_{-n},\mathbf{0})}(\mathbf{B})-L_{(-n,t_{-n},(k,-k)}(\mathbf{B})=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\eta}(\mathbf{0},(k,-k)),\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{(k,-k),(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{B})-L_{\mathbf{0},(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{B})=\mathcal{B}^{\theta}((k,-k),\mathbf{0}).

Then, we have the following inclusion of sets:

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\text{There exists a sequence }\{t_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\text{ satisfying }\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{n}}{n}=\theta\text{ and }\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{-n}}{-n}=\eta

\displaystyle\qquad\text{such that, for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\text{ some geodesic between }(-n,t_{-n})\text{ and }(n,t_{n})\text{ passes through }\mathbf{0}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcap_{m=1}^{\infty}\Bigg\{\text{There exists a sequence }\{t_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\text{ satisfying }\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{n}}{n}=\theta\text{ and }\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{-n}}{-n}=\eta\text{ such that }

\displaystyle\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\text{ and }k=1,\ldots,m,\;L_{(-n,t_{-n}),\mathbf{0}}(\mathbf{B})-L_{(-n,t_{-n}),(k,-k)}(\mathbf{B})\geq L_{(k,-k),(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{B})-L_{\mathbf{0},(n,t_{n})}(\mathbf{B})\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcap_{m=1}^{\infty}\bigl\{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\eta}(\mathbf{0},(k,-k))\geq\mathcal{B}^{\theta}((k,-k),\mathbf{0}),\;\;k=1,\ldots,m\bigr\}\cup\Omega_{\mathbb{Z}}^{C}.

We show that this last event has probability
0
.
Define the process
\{S_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
by
S_{0}=0
and for
k\geq 1
,

S_{k}=\mathcal{B}^{\eta}(\mathbf{0},(k,-k))-\mathcal{B}^{\theta}((k,-k),\mathbf{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\eta}((i-1,-i+1),(i,-i))-\mathcal{B}^{\theta}\bigl((i,-i),(i-1,-i+1)\bigr)\bigr).

The sequences

\bigl\{\mathcal{B}^{\theta}((i,-i),(i-1,-i+1))\bigr\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigl\{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\eta}((i-1,-i+1),(i,-i))\bigr\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}

are independent because they are constructed from disjoint increments of the field of independent Brownian motions
\mathbf{B}
. Furthermore,

\bigl\{\mathcal{B}^{\theta}((i,-i),(i-1,-i+1))\bigr\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}=\bigl\{v_{i}^{\theta}(-i)+h_{i-1}^{\theta}(-i,-i+1)\bigr\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}

is an i.i.d. collection of random variables by Theorem
3.7
(iv)
. By reflection applied to the Busemann functions
\widetilde{B}^{\eta}
, the process
\{S_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
has independent increments. By Theorem
3.7
, the increments of this random walk are independent sums of normal and exponential random variables and therefore have finite first and second moment. Let
\mu
be the mean and
\sigma^{2}
the variance of
S_{1}
. Then,
\mu\leq 0
by the assumption (
7.28
). Thus, for
m\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\eta}\bigl(\mathbf{0},(k,-k)\bigr)\geq\mathcal{B}^{\theta}\bigl((k,-k),\mathbf{0}\bigr),\;\;k=1,\ldots,m\bigr)=\mathbb{P}(S_{k}\geq 0,\;\;k=1,\ldots,m)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{S_{\lfloor um\rfloor}-um\mu}{\sqrt{m\sigma^{2}}}\geq-\frac{um\mu}{\sqrt{m\sigma^{2}}}\;\text{for }u\in[0,1]\biggr)\leq\mathbb{P}\biggl(\inf_{u\in[0,1]}\frac{S_{\lfloor um\rfloor}-um\mu}{\sqrt{m\sigma^{2}}}\geq 0\biggr)\overset{m\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

The convergence in the last step holds because

\inf_{u\in[0,1]}\frac{S_{\lfloor um\rfloor}-um\mu}{\sqrt{m\sigma^{2}}}\overset{m\rightarrow\infty}{\Longrightarrow}\inf_{u\in[0,1]}B(u)

where the convergence holds in distribution by Donsker’s Theorem, and
B
is a standard Brownian motion.
∎

We are now ready to prove all parts of the main theorem of this paper.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Unless specified otherwise, the full probability event of the parts of this theorem is the event
\Omega_{2}
defined in Equation (
7.10
).

Part
(i)
:
This is a direct corollary of Theorem
4.3
(i)
.

Part
(ii)
:

Let
\Omega_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}
be the event defined in Equation (
7.15
). By Lemma
7.6

on
\Omega_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}
, there is a unique element of
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta}
. Setting
\mathbf{x}=(m,t)
,
\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta-,L}=\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta+,R}
for all
r\geq m
, and the desired conclusion follows from Theorem
4.5
(ii)
.

Part
(iii)
:
By Theorems
3.5
(viii)
and
4.5
(ii)
, on the full probability event
\Omega^{(\theta)}\cap\Omega_{2}
, there exists multiple
\theta
-directed semi-infinite geodesics from
(m,t)
if and only if
\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}<\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,R}
for some
r\geq m
, or in other words, if and only if
\mathbf{T}_{(m,t)}^{\theta}
contains more than one element. Since the event
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
constructed in Equation (
7.16
) is contained in
\Omega^{(\theta)}
, Theorem
4.7
(i)
implies that this set of points whose
\theta
-directed geodesic is not unique is countable. Then, since
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}\subseteq\bigcap_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Q}}\Omega_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\theta)}
, if two
\theta
-directed geodesics from
(m,t)
pass through
(m,t+\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
, then they both pass through
(m,q)
for some
(m,q)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Q}
. Further, the portions of the geodesics after
(m,q)
are both
\theta
-directed semi-infinite geodesics from
(m,q)
and are therefore the same geodesic by Part
(ii)
.

Part
(iv)
: Assume to the contrary, that, for some
\omega\in\Omega_{2}
, there exists a point
(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
and a sequence
t=t_{m-1}\leq t_{m}\leq t_{m+1}\leq\cdots
defining a semi-infinite geodesic
\Gamma
, starting from
(m,t)
and satisfying

0\leq\underline{\theta}:=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{n}}{n}<\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{n}}{n}=:\overline{\theta}\leq\infty.

Choose some
\theta\in(\underline{\theta},\overline{\theta})
. By Theorem
4.3
(v)
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta-,L}}{n}=\theta

Therefore, there are infinitely many values of
n
with
t_{n}<\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta-,L}
and another infinitely many values of
n
with
t_{n}>\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta-,L}
. Let
n_{1}
be the minimal index such that
t_{n_{1}}<\tau_{(m,t),n_{1}}^{\theta-,L}
, and let
n_{2}
be the minimal index larger than
n_{1}
such that
t_{n_{2}}>\tau_{(m,t),n_{2}}^{\theta-,L}
. See Figure
7.6
for clarity. By planarity, the points
(n_{1},t_{1})
and
(n_{2},\tau_{(m,t),n_{2}-1}^{\theta-,L})
lie on both geodesics, and between the two points, the path
\Gamma
lies strictly to the left of the leftmost geodesic in
\mathbf{T}_{(m,t)}^{\theta-}
. This contradicts Theorem
4.3
(ii)
which states the leftmost geodesic in
\mathbf{T}_{(m,t)}^{\theta-}
is the leftmost geodesic between any two of its points.

(m,t)
(n_{2},\tau_{(m,t),n_{2}}^{\theta-,L})
(n_{2},t_{n_{2}})
(n_{1},t_{n_{1}})
(n_{2},\tau_{(m,t),n_{2}-1}^{\theta-,L})
(n_{1},\tau_{(m,t),n_{1}}^{\theta-,L})

Figure 7.6.
Crossing of geodesic paths. The blue/thin path is the semi-infinite geodesic defined by the jump times
t\leq t_{m}\leq t_{m+1}\leq\cdots
, and the red/thick path is the semi-infinite geodesic defined by the times
t\leq\tau_{(m,t),m}^{\theta-,L}\leq\tau_{(m,t),m+1}^{\theta-,L}\leq\cdots
.

Part
(v)
:
Next, assume that for some
\omega\in\Omega_{2}
, there exists
(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, and a sequence
t=t_{m-1}\leq t_{m}\leq\cdots
that defines a semi-infinite geodesic
\Gamma
, starting from
(m,t)
and satisfying

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{n}}{n}=0.

To show that
t_{r}=t
for all
r\geq m
, it is sufficient to show that
t_{m}=t
. For then, this semi-infinite geodesic travels vertically to
(m+1,t)
, the remaining part of the geodesic is a semi-infinite geodesic starting at
(m+1,t)
, and the result follows by induction. By Theorem
4.3
(v)
, for every
\theta>0
and any
(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta+,R}}{n}=\theta.

Hence, for all
\theta>0
and all sufficiently large
n
,
t_{n}<\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta+,R}
. However, by an analogous argument as in Part
(iv)
, again using Theorem
2.3
(ii)
,
t_{r}\leq\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta+,R}
for all
r\geq m
. Specifically, the inequality holds for
r=m
. By monotonicity of Theorem
4.3
(iii)
(iii)(a)
,

\lim_{\theta\searrow 0}\tau_{(m,t),m}^{\theta+,R}
exists.
By definition of the event
\Omega_{1}
from Lemma
6.4
, this limit equals
t
on the event
\Omega_{1}\supseteq\Omega_{2}
. The case where
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{t_{n}}{n}=\infty
is handled similarly.

Part
(vi)
:
Let
\Omega^{(\theta,\eta)}
be a full probability event on which, for every
(m,q)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Q}
, there exists no sequences satisfying the conditions (
3.2
) and such that, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists a geodesic between
(-n,\tau_{-n})
and
(n,\tau_{n})
that passes through
(m,q)
. Such an event exists by Lemma
3.3
. Then, on this event,
if a bi-infinite geodesic that satisfies (
3.2
) exists, it cannot
pass through
(m,q)
for any
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. But then this bi-infinite geodesic cannot ever move horizontally, and so there must exist
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that the bi-infinite geodesic consists only of points
(r,t)
. Now (
3.2
) fails.

Part
(vii)
:

Define

\widehat{\Omega}^{(\theta)}=\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}\cap\Omega^{(\theta,\star)}\cap\Omega^{(\theta,\theta)}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta}),

where
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
is the event defined in (
7.16
),
\Omega^{(\theta,\star)}
is the event of Theorem
7.11
(v)
, and
\Omega^{(\theta,\theta)}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta})
is the event of Part
(vi)
, applied to the random environment
\mathbf{X}^{\theta}
of i.i.d Brownian motions (Lemma
3.7
(ii)
).

By the construction of the
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta}
in terms of the variational formula (Definition
4.1
), if for some
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, the rightmost semi-infinite geodesics in
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta}
and
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{y}}^{\theta}
ever intersect, they agree above and to the right of the point of intersection. The same is true of leftmost geodesics. Because
\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}\subseteq\Omega^{(\theta)}\cap\Omega_{2}
(see discussion after (
7.16
)),

\widehat{\Omega}^{(\theta)}\subseteq\Omega^{(\theta)}\cap\Omega^{(\theta,\star)}\cap\Omega_{2}.

If, by way of contradiction, for some
\omega\in\widehat{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
, there exists
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
such that the rightmost geodesics in
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\theta}
and
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{y}}^{\theta}
are disjoint, then by Theorem
4.9
, there exists a bi-infinite geodesic for the environment
\mathbf{X}^{\theta}
that is defined by jump times
\{\tau_{r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
and satisfies

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{n}}{n}=\theta=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{-n}}{-n}.

This is a contradiction since
\omega\in\Omega^{(\theta,\theta)}(\mathbf{X}^{\theta})
. Therefore, for all
\omega\in\widehat{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
, whenever
(m,s),(k,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
, the rightmost geodesics in
\mathbf{T}_{(m,s)}^{\theta}
and
\mathbf{T}_{(r,t)}^{\theta}
coalesce. The same is true by replacing “right” with “left.” In other words, for all sufficiently large
r
,

(7.29)

\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,R}=\tau_{(k,t),r}^{\theta,R}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,L}=\tau_{(k,t),r}^{\theta,L}.

Now, let
s=s_{m-1}\leq s_{m}\leq\cdots
and
t=t_{k-1}\leq t_{k}\leq\cdots
be any sequences defining
\theta
-directed semi-infinite geodesics starting from
(m,s)
and
(k,t)
, respectively. We show that these semi-infinite geodesics coalesce. Without loss of generality, assume that
m\leq k
and
s_{k-1}<t_{k-1}=t
. The other cases are handled similarly. Then, by Theorems
4.5
(ii)
and
4.3
(iii)
(iii)(c)
, for all
r\geq k
,

\tau_{(k,s_{k-1}),r}^{\theta,L}\leq s_{r}\leq\tau_{(k,s_{k-1}),r}^{\theta,R}\leq\tau_{(k,t),r}^{\theta,L}\leq t_{r}\leq\tau_{(k,t),r}^{\theta,R}.

Then, by (
7.29
), these inequalities are all equalities for all sufficiently large
r
, and the geodesics coalesce.
∎

We conclude this section by completing the remaining parts of Theorem
4.3
.

Proof of remaining parts of Theorem
4.3
:

Part
(iii)
(iii)(c)
:
Let
\omega\in\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
s<t\in\mathbb{R}
. We use a modified induction, in the following manner.

(A)

First, note by definition that
s=\tau_{(m,s),m-1}^{\theta,R}<\tau_{(m,t),m-1}^{\theta,L}=t
.

(B)

For each
r\geq m
, we assume that
\tau_{(m,s),r-1}^{\theta,R}<\tau_{(m,t),r-1}^{\theta,L}
.

(C)

Under assumption
(B)
, we show that if
\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,R}\geq\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}
, then
\tau_{(m,s),k}^{\theta,R}=\tau_{(m,t),k}^{\theta,L}
for all
k\geq r
.

With this procedure mapped out, assume that, for some
r\geq m
,

\tau_{(m,s),r-1}^{\theta,R}<\tau_{(m,t),r-1}^{\theta,L}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,R}\geq\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}.

By definition,
\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,R}
is a maximizer of
B_{r}(u)-h_{r+1}^{\theta}(u)
over
u\in[\tau_{(m,s),r-1}^{\theta,R},\infty)
. But since

\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,R}\geq\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}\geq\tau_{(m,t),r-1}^{\theta,L},

\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,R}
is also a maximizer over
u\in[\tau_{(m,t),r-1}^{\theta,L},\infty)
. By definition,
\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}
is another maximizer over this set, so

B_{r}(\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,R})-h_{r+1}^{\theta}(\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,R})=B_{r}(\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L})-h_{r+1}^{\theta}(\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}),

and both
\tau_{(m,s),r}^{\theta,R}
and
\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta,L}
are maximizers of
B_{r}(u)-h_{r+1}^{\theta}(u)
over
u\in[q,\infty)
for any rational
q\in[\tau_{(m,s),r-1}^{\theta,R},\tau_{(m,t),r-1}^{\theta,L}]
. By assumption
(B)
, such a rational
q
exists. Then, the sequence
\{\tau_{(m,s),k}^{\theta,R}\}_{k\geq r}
and the sequence
\{\tau_{(m,t),k}^{\theta,L}\}_{k\geq r}
both define jump times for a semi-infinite geodesic starting from
(r,q)
. Since
\omega\in\widetilde{\Omega}^{(\theta)}
, there is a unique
\theta
-directed semi-infinite geodesic starting from this point, and conclusion
(C)
holds.

Part
(iv)
(iv)(b)
: By the monotonicity of Theorem
4.3
(iii)
(iii)(a)
, it suffices to show that

\lim_{\theta\searrow 0}\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta+,R}=t\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{\theta\rightarrow\infty}\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta-,L}=\infty\qquad\text{for }r\geq m.

The proof of Theorem
3.1
(v)
, established the statement for
r=m
. For the limits as
\theta\rightarrow\infty
, this implies the statement holds for all
r\geq m
since
\tau_{(m,t),m}^{\theta-,L}\leq\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta-,L}
. By the monotonicity of Theorem
4.3
(iii)
(iii)(a)
, the limit

\tau_{(m,t),r}^{0}:=\lim_{\theta\searrow 0}\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta+,R}

exists and satisfies
\tau_{(m,t),r}^{0}\leq\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta+,R}
for all
\theta>0
. Furthermore, since
\{\tau_{(m,t),r}^{\theta+,R}\}_{r\geq m-1}
is a nondecreasing sequence for each
\theta>0
, the sequence
\{\tau_{(m,t),r}^{0}\}_{r\geq m-1}
is also nondecreasing. By Theorem
4.3
(v)
,

0\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{(m,t),n}^{0}}{n}\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta+,R}}{n}=\theta\qquad\text{ for all }\theta>0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\tau_{(m,t),n}^{0}}{n}=0.

Therefore, by Theorem
3.1
(v)
, if the sequence of jump times
t=\tau_{(m,t),m-1}^{0,R}\leq\tau_{(m,t),m}^{0,R}\leq\cdots
defines a semi-infinite geodesic starting from
(m,t)
, the desired conclusion follows. It is sufficient to show that, for any
n\geq m
, the sequence
t=\tau_{(m,t),m-1}^{0}\leq\tau_{(m,t),m}^{0}\leq\cdots\leq\tau_{(m,t),n}^{0}
defines jump times for a finite geodesic between
(m,t)
and
(n,\tau_{(m,t),n}^{0})
. By Theorem
4.3
(i)
, For each
\theta>0
, the sequence
t=\tau_{(m,t),m-1}^{\theta+,R}\leq\cdots\leq\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta+,R}
is a maximizing sequence for

L_{(m,t),(n,\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta+,R})}=\sup\Bigl\{\sum_{r=m}^{n}B_{r}(s_{r},s_{r-1}):t=s_{m-1}\leq s_{m}\leq\cdots\leq s_{n}=\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta+,R}\Bigr\},

so since
\lim_{\theta\searrow 0}\tau_{(m,t),n}^{\theta+,R}=\tau_{(m,t),n}^{0}
, Lemma
A.3
completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Deterministic facts about continuous functions

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\eta^{1},\eta^{2}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be continuous functions satisfying

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\eta^{i}(t)=-\infty\qquad\text{for }i=1,2.

Further, assume that
\eta^{1}(s,u)\leq\eta^{2}(s,u)
for all
s<u
. For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
i=1,2
, let
s_{t}^{i,L}
and
s_{t}^{i,R}
be the leftmost and rightmost maximizers of
\eta^{i}
on the set
[t,\infty)
. Then,

s_{t}^{1,L}\leq s_{t}^{2,L}\qquad\text{and}\qquad s_{t}^{1,R}\leq s_{t}^{2,R}.

Proof.

Since
s_{t}^{2,L}
is a maximizer for
\eta^{2}
on
[t,\infty)
,
\eta^{2}(s_{t}^{2,L})\geq\eta^{2}(s)
, or equivalently,
\eta^{2}(s_{t}^{2,L},s)\leq 0
for all
s\geq t
, Then, for all
s\geq s_{t}^{2,L}\geq t
, the hypothesis of the lemma gives

\eta^{1}(s_{t}^{2,L},s)\leq\eta^{2}(s_{t}^{2,L},s)\leq 0.

Thus, the leftmost maximum of
\eta^{1}
on the larger set
[t,\infty)
can be no larger than
s_{t}^{2,L}
.

For rightmost maximizers, the lemma follows by similar reasoning: for
s>s_{t}^{2,R}
,

\eta^{1}(s_{t}^{2,R},s)\leq\eta^{2}(s_{t}^{2,R},s)<0,

so
\eta^{1}(s_{t}^{2,R})>\eta^{1}(s)
for all
s>s_{t}^{2,R}
, and no maximizer of
\eta^{1}(s)
over
s\in[t,\infty)
can be larger than
s_{t}^{2,R}
.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
f_{n}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of continuous functions, converging uniformly to the function
f:S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Assume that there exists a sequence
\{c_{n}\}
, of maximizers of
f_{n}
, converging to some
c\in S
. Then,
c
is a maximizer of
f
.

Proof.

f_{n}(c_{n})\geq f_{n}(x)
for all
x\in S
, so it suffices to show that
f_{n}(c_{n})\rightarrow f(c)
. This follows from the uniform convergence of
f_{n}
to
f
, the continuity of
f
, and

|f_{n}(c_{n})-f(c)|\leq|f_{n}(c_{n})-f(c_{n})|+|f(c_{n})-f(c)|.\qed

Lemma A.3
.

Let
S_{n}
for
n\geq 0
be subsets of some set
\widetilde{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, on which the function
f:\widetilde{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is continuous. Assume that each point
x\in S_{0}
is the limit of a sequence
\{x_{n}\}
, where
x_{n}\in S_{n}
for each
n
. Assume that
\{c_{n}\}
is a sequence of maximizers of
f
on
S_{n}
. Assume further that
c_{n}
converges to some
c\in S_{0}
. Then,
c
is a maximizer of
f
on
S_{0}
.

Proof.

For each
x_{0}\in S_{0}
, write
x_{0}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}x_{n}
, where
x_{n}\in S_{n}
for each
n
. Then,
f(c_{n})\geq f(x_{n})
for all
n\geq 1
, and the result follows by taking limits.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\mathbf{X}=\{X_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
, where each
X_{m}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function. Let
0<s<t<T<u
and
m\leq n
. Then,

X_{m}(s,t)\leq L_{(m,s),(n,u)}(\mathbf{X})-L_{(m,t),(n,u)}(\mathbf{X})\leq L_{(m,s),(n,T)}(\mathbf{X})-L_{(m,t),(n,T)}(\mathbf{X})

Similarly, let
0<s<t<u<\infty
and
m<n
. Then,

0\leq L_{(m,s),(n,t)}(\mathbf{X})-L_{(m+1,s),(n,t)}(\mathbf{X})\leq L_{(m,s),(n,u)}(\mathbf{X})-L_{(m+1,s),(n,u)}(\mathbf{X}).

Remark A.5
.

This is a deterministic statement. The only necessary ingredient is the continuity of the
X_{m}
so that each of the last-passage times is finite and has a sequence of maximizing times.

Proof.

This proof follows a standard paths-crossing argument. For example, the proofs of Lemma 4.6 in
[
ARAS20
]
and Proposition 3.8 in
[
DOV18
]
follow the same procedure. We prove the first statement, and the second is proven similarly. By definition of last-passage time,

X_{m}(s,t)+L_{(m,t),(n,u)}\leq L_{(m,s),(n,u)}.

Since
0<s<t<T<u
, any geodesic between
(m,s)
and
(n,u)
must cross any geodesic between
(m,t)
and
(n,T)
. Let
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
be a point of intersection. Then,

L_{(m,s),(n,u)}=L_{(m,s),\mathbf{z}}+L_{\mathbf{z},(n,u)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad L_{(m,t),(n,T)}=L_{(m,t),\mathbf{z}}+L_{\mathbf{z},(n,T)},

so

\displaystyle L_{(m,s),(n,u)}-L_{(m,t),(n,u)}\leq L_{(m,s),\mathbf{z}}+L_{\mathbf{z},(n,u)}-\bigl(L_{(m,t),\mathbf{z}}+L_{\mathbf{z},(n,u)}\bigr)=L_{(m,s),\mathbf{z}}-L_{(m,t),\mathbf{z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L_{(m,s),\mathbf{z}}+L_{\mathbf{z},(n,T)}-\bigl(L_{(m,t),\mathbf{z}}+L_{\mathbf{z},(n,T)}\bigr)\leq L_{(m,s),(n,T)}-L_{(m,t),(n,T)}.\qed

Appendix B
Probabilistic results

The following is a classical result that is often used in this paper.

Lemma B.1

(
[
BS02
]
, Equation 1.1.4 (1) on pg 251)
.

For a standard Brownian motion
B
and
\lambda>0
,

\sup_{0\leq s<\infty}\{\sqrt{2}B(s)-\lambda s\}\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda).

Theorem B.2
.

Let
B
be a standard Brownian motion, and for
t>0
, let

\displaystyle D(t)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{0\leq s<\infty}\bigl\{\sqrt{2}B(s)-\lambda s\bigr\}-\sup_{t\leq s<\infty}\bigl\{\sqrt{2}B(s)-\lambda s\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\bigl\{\sqrt{2}B(t,s)+\lambda(t-s)\bigr\}-\sup_{t\leq s<\infty}\bigl\{\sqrt{2}B(t,s)+\lambda(t-s)\bigr\}\bigr)^{+}.

Then, for all
z\geq 0
,

(B.1)

\mathbb{P}(D(t)\leq z)=\Phi\bigl(\frac{z-\lambda t}{\sqrt{2t}}\bigr)+e^{\lambda z}\left((1+\lambda z+\lambda^{2}t)\Phi\bigl(-\frac{z+\lambda t}{\sqrt{2t}}\bigr)-\lambda\sqrt{\frac{t}{\pi}}e^{-\frac{(z+\lambda t)^{2}}{4t}}\right).

Proof.

For
x\geq 0
, by time reversal and
[
BS02
]
, Equation 1.2.4 on page 251,

\displaystyle\quad\;\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\{\sqrt{2}B(t,s)+\lambda(t-s)\}\leq x\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\{\sqrt{2}B(t,t-s)-\lambda(t-s-t)\}\leq x\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\{\sqrt{2}B(s)+\lambda s\}\leq x\bigr)=\Phi\bigl(\frac{x-\lambda t}{\sqrt{2t}}\bigr)-e^{\lambda x}\Phi\bigl(\frac{-x-\lambda t}{\sqrt{2t}}\bigr).

By Lemma
B.1
,

\sup_{t\leq s<\infty}\{\sqrt{2}B(t,s)+\lambda(t-s)\}\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda).

The conclusion of the theorem follows by a simple, but tedious convolution. It suffices to show the
t=1
case, and the general case follows by Brownian scaling.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(D(1)\leq z)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\left(\Phi\bigl(\frac{z-y-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)-e^{\lambda(z-y)}\Phi\bigl(\frac{-z+y-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)\right)\lambda e^{\lambda y}\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{z-y-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}e^{\lambda y}\,dx\,dy-e^{\lambda z}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{-z+y-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx\,dy.

We now use Fubini’s Theorem to switch the order of integration. This results in

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{z-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}e^{\lambda y}\,dy\,dx+\int_{\frac{z-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{z-\lambda-\sqrt{2}x}\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}e^{\lambda y}\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-e^{\lambda z}\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{z+\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}\int_{\sqrt{2}x+z+\lambda}^{0}\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{z-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx+\int_{\frac{z-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}e^{z\lambda-\sqrt{2}\lambda x-\lambda^{2}}\,dx+e^{\lambda z}\int_{-\infty}^{\frac{-z-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}(\sqrt{2}x+z+\lambda)\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\Phi\bigl(\frac{z-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)+e^{\lambda z}\int_{\frac{z-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(x+\sqrt{2}\lambda)^{2}}{2}}\,dx+e^{\lambda z}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{z+\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{\pi}}xe^{-x^{2}/2}\,dx+(\lambda z+\lambda^{2})\Phi\bigl(-\frac{z+\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\Phi\bigl(\frac{z-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)+e^{\lambda z}\int_{\frac{z+\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-u^{2}/2}\,du+e^{\lambda z}\left(-\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{(z+\lambda)^{2}}{4}}+(\lambda z+\lambda^{2})\Phi\bigl(-\frac{z+\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\Phi\bigl(\frac{z-\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)+e^{\lambda z}\left((1+\lambda z+\lambda^{2})\Phi\bigl(-\frac{z+\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\bigr)-\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{(z+\lambda)^{2}}{4}}\right).\qed

Theorem B.3
.

Let
B
be a standard Brownian motion and let
\lambda>0
. Let
T
be the unique maximizer of
\sqrt{2}B(t)-\lambda t
for
t\in[0,\infty)
. Then, for
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}(T>t)=(2+\lambda^{2}t)\Phi\left(-\lambda\sqrt{{t}/{2}}\,\right)-\lambda\sqrt{{t}/{\pi}\,}\,e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}t}{4}}.

Remark B.4
.

Theorem
B.3
should be credited to Norros and Salminen, who computed the Laplace transform of this random variable in Proposition 3.9 of
[
SN01
]
. One can also obtain by integrating the probability density of the time of maximum of Brownian motion with drift on the interval
[0,t]
found in
[
Buf03
]
, Equation (1.3), and then taking
t\rightarrow\infty
. See also the discussion after Equation (1.3) equation in
[
Buf03
]
for more historical details on this formula. Theorem
B.2
will be used in subsequent papers, so we use it to give our own proof of Theorem
B.3
.

Proof of Theorem
B.3
.

Note that
T>t
if and only if
D(t)=0
in the sense of Theorem
B.2
. Thus, the Lemma follows by setting
z=0
in the formula (
B.1
).
∎

Theorem B.5
.

Let
X
be a two-sided Brownian motion with strictly negative drift. Let

M=\{t\in\mathbb{R}:X(t)=\sup_{t\leq s<\infty}X(s)\}.

Furthermore, let

M^{U}=\big\{t\in M:X(t)>X(s)\text{ for all }s>t\big\}

be the set of
t\in M
that are unique maximizers of
X(s)
over
s\in[t,\infty)
. Define

M^{N}=M\setminus M^{U}
to be the set of
t\in M
that are non-unique maximizers of
X(s)
over
s\in[t,\infty)
.
Then, there exists an event of probability one, on which the following hold.

(i)

M
is a closed set.

(ii)

For all
\hat{t}\in M^{U}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
t\in M^{N}
satisfying
\hat{t}<t<\hat{t}+\varepsilon
. For all
t\in M^{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\hat{t}\in M^{U}
satisfying
t-\varepsilon<\hat{t}<t
.

(iii)

For all
t\in M^{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
t^{\star}\in M^{N}
with
t-\varepsilon<t^{\star}<t
. For each
t\in M^{N}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
M\cap(t,t+\delta)=\varnothing
.

(iv)

M^{N}
is a countably infinite set.

Proof.

By the
n=m
case of Lemma
7.1
, for each fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
X(s)
is almost surely uniquely maximized for
s\in[t,\infty)
. By Theorem
B.3
, this maximizer is almost surely strictly greater than
t
. Let
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}}
be the full probability event on which,

\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}X(s)=\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}X(s)=-\infty,

and such that, for every
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, there is a unique maximizer of
X(s)
over
s\in[q,\infty)
that is strictly larger than
q
.

Part
(i)
: This follows from Lemma
A.3
.

Part
(ii)
: Let
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\hat{t}\in M^{U}
and
\varepsilon>0
, and let
q\in\mathbb{Q}
satisfy
\hat{t}<q<\hat{t}+\varepsilon
. Then, there is a unique maximizer,
s>q
of
X(u)
over
u\in[q,\infty)
. By assumption,
\hat{t}
uniquely maximizes
X(u)
over the larger set
[\hat{t},\infty)
, so
X(q)<X(s)<X(\hat{t})
. By the intermediate value theorem, there exists a point
u
with
\hat{t}<u<q
and
X(u)=X(s)
. Then, set

t=\max\{u\in(\hat{t},s):X(u)=X(s)\}.

Then,
t<q<s
, and

X(t)=X(s)=\sup_{t\leq u<\infty}X(u)

because
X(u)<X(s)
for all
t<u<q
. Therefore,
\hat{t}<t<q<\hat{t}+\varepsilon
, and
t\in M^{N}
.

Now, let
t\in M^{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}
satisfy
t-\varepsilon<q<t
. Then, there is a unique maximizer
\hat{t}
of
X(s)
over
s\in[q,\infty)
, so
X(\hat{t})>X(s)
for all
s\in[q,\infty)\backslash\{\hat{t}\}
. Specifically,
\hat{t}\in M^{U}
and
X(\hat{t})>X(t)
, so since
t
maximizes
X(s)
over
s\in[t,\infty)
,
\hat{t}
cannot be greater than or equal to
t
. Therefore,
t-\varepsilon<q<\hat{t}<t
.

Part
(iii)
: The first statement follows immediately from Part
(ii)
. We now prove the second statement. We start by showing that for each
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}}
, there does not exist
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
X(s)
has three maximizers over
s\in[t,\infty)
. If, on the contrary, such a value of
t
exists, at least two of the maximizers must be greater than
t
, and therefore, these two maximizers are also maximizers of
X(s)
over
s\in[q,\infty)
for some
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. Thus, on
\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}}
, for each
t\in M^{N}
, there exists a unique
\hat{t}\in M^{U}
such that
\hat{t}>t
and
(t,\hat{t})\cap M=\varnothing.

Part
(iv)
: The set
M
is nonempty because, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, any maximizer
t
of
X(u)
over
u\in[s,\infty)
lies in
M
. Then, by the first statement of Part
(iii)
, the set
M^{N}
is infinite. By the second statement of Part
(iii)
,
M^{N}
is countable.
∎

Remark B.6
.

Originally discovered by Taylor
[
Tay55
]
, it is well known that the zero set of Brownian motion almost surely has Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
. Parts
(i)
and
(ii)
of Theorem
B.5
imply that
M
is the supporting set of the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure defined by the nondecreasing function
t\mapsto-\sup_{t\leq s<\infty}X(s)
. Using this fact, Taylor’s proof (see also Theorem 4.24 in
[
MP10
]
) can be modified to show that the set
M
almost surely has Hausdorff dimension
\frac{1}{2}
.

Appendix C
The Brownian queue and stationary last-passage process

This section discusses the Brownian queue in the formulation of
[
OY01
]
. Let
A
and
S
be two independent, two-sided Brownian motions, and let
\lambda>0
. For
s<t
,
A(s,t)
represents the arrivals to the queue in the time interval
(s,t]
, and
\lambda(t-s)-S(s,t)
is the amount of service available in
(s,t]
. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, set

q(t)=\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}\{A(s,t)+S(s,t)-\lambda(t-s)\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad d(t)=A(t)+q(0)-q(t).

In queuing terms,
q(t)
is the length of the queue at time
t
, and for
s<t
,
d(s,t)
is the number of departures from the queue in the interval
(s,t]
. These processes are not integer-valued, but are viewed as heavy-traffic limits. We also define
e(t)=S(t)+q(0)-q(t)
. The following is due to O’Connell and Yor
[
OY01
]
. Without the statements for the process
e
the theorem is a special case of a more general result previously shown by Harrison and Williams
[
HW90
]
.

Theorem C.1

(
[
OY01
]
, Theorem 4)
.

The processes
d
and
e
are independent, two-sided Brownian motions. Furthermore, for each
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\{d(s,t),e(s,t):-\infty<s\leq t\}
is independent of
\{q(u):u\geq t\}
.

We reformulate Theorem
C.1
in terms of the queuing mappings of (
3.3
)–(
3.5
) and (
3.6
)–(
3.8
).

Theorem C.2
.

Let
Y
be a two-sided Brownian motion with drift
\lambda>0
, independent of the two-sided Brownian motion
C
(with no drift). Then,
\overleftarrow{D}(Y,C)
is a two-sided Brownian motion with drift
\lambda
, independent of the two-sided Brownian motion
\overleftarrow{R}(Y,C)
. Furthermore, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\{(\overleftarrow{D}(Y,C)(s,t),\overleftarrow{R}(Y,C)(s,t)):-\infty<s\leq t\}
is independent of
\{\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(u):u\geq t\}
.

Proof.

Let
A(t)=\lambda t-Y(t)
and
S(t)=C(t)
, two independent two-sided Brownian motions. Then

\displaystyle q(t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}\{A(s,t)+S(s,t)-\lambda(t-s)\}=\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}\{C(s,t)-Y(s,t)\}=\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(t).

Next observe that

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{D}(Y,C)(t)

\displaystyle=Y(t)+\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(t)-\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(0)=-A(t)+\lambda t+q(t)-q(0)=\lambda t-d(t),

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\overleftarrow{R}(Y,C)(t)

\displaystyle=C(t)+\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(0)-\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(t)=S(t)+q(0)-q(t)=e(t).

The result follows from Theorem
C.1
.
∎

Fix a parameter
\lambda>0
. Given an environment of Brownian motions
\mathbf{B}=\{B_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
, set

Y_{0}^{\lambda}(t)=-B_{0}(t)+\lambda t.

For
m>0
, recalling definitions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
), set

(C.1)

q_{m}^{\lambda}(t):=\overleftarrow{Q}(Y_{m-1}^{\lambda},B_{m})(t),\;\;Y_{m}^{\lambda}(t):=\overleftarrow{D}(Y_{m-1}^{\lambda},B_{m})(t),\;\;\text{and}\;\;W_{m-1}^{\lambda}(t):=\overleftarrow{R}(Y_{m-1}^{\lambda},B_{m})(t).

The increment-stationary BLPP is constructed as follows: For
(n,t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}
, set

L^{\lambda}_{(n,t)}=\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}\{Y_{0}^{\lambda}(s)+L_{(1,s),(n,t)}(\mathbf{B})\}.

One can check inductively, as in Section 4 of
[
OY01
]
, that for
m\geq 1
and
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
,

q_{m}^{\lambda}(t)=L^{\lambda}_{(m,t)}-L^{\lambda}_{(m-1,t)},\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;Y_{m}^{\lambda}(s,t)=L^{\lambda}_{(m,t)}-L^{\lambda}_{(m,s)}.

The stationarity of these increments comes from the next theorem that follows from Theorem
C.2
and induction. It is the zero-temperature analogue of Theorem 3.3 in
[
SV10
]
and Theorem 2.11 in
[
ARAS20
]
. Figure
C.1
demonstrates the independence structure, and we give credit to
[
ARAS20
]
for a very similar picture.

0

n-3

n

q_{1}^{\lambda}

W_{n-3}^{\lambda}
\ddots
q^{\lambda}_{n-1}
Y^{\lambda}_{n-2}
W^{\lambda}_{n-2}
B_{n-2}

W_{n-1}^{\lambda}
Y^{\lambda}_{n-1}

B_{n}
t_{n}

\cdots
t_{3}

Figure C.1.
Independence structure for stationary BLPP. Each process
Y^{\lambda}_{r}
is associated to the (purple) dashed segment on level
r
, processes
B_{r}
and
W^{\lambda}_{r}
cover the remaining portions of horizontal level
r
, and the process
q_{r}^{\lambda}
is associated to the (red) vertical edge from level
r-1
to
r
at time point
t_{r}
.

Theorem C.3
.

Let
Y_{m}^{\lambda},q_{m}^{\lambda},W_{m}^{\lambda}
be as constructed in (
C.1
). Then, the following hold.

(i)

For all
m>0
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
q_{m}^{\lambda}(t)\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda)
.
\{W_{m}^{\lambda}\}_{m\geq 0}
is a field of independent two-sided Brownian motions.
\{Y_{m}^{\lambda}\}_{m\geq 0}
is a field of (non-independent) two-sided Brownian motions with drift
\lambda
.

(ii)

Let
-\infty<t_{n}\leq t_{n-1}\leq\cdots\leq t_{1}<\infty
. Then, the following are mutually independent:

\{W_{0}^{\lambda}(u,t_{1}):u\leq t_{1}\},\;\;q_{1}^{\lambda}(t_{1}),\;\;\{Y_{0}^{\lambda}(t_{1},u):u\geq t_{1}\},\;\\[10.00002pt]
\{W_{r}^{\lambda}(u,t_{r+1}):u\leq t_{r+1}\},\;q_{r+1}^{\lambda}(t_{r+1}),\;\{Y_{r}^{\lambda}(t_{r+1},u):t_{r+1}\leq u\leq t_{r}\},\;\\[10.00002pt]
\text{and}\;\{B_{r}(t_{r},u):u\geq t_{r}\},\;\text{ for }\;1\leq r\leq n-1,\;\;\{Y_{n}^{\lambda}(u,t_{n}):u\leq t_{n}\},\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\{B_{n}(t_{n},u):u\geq t_{n}\}.

Proof.

To prove Part (i), begin with the assumption:

(Y_{0}^{\lambda},B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\qquad\text{are mutually independent}.

By Theorem
C.2
,
Y_{1}^{\lambda}=\overleftarrow{D}(Y_{0}^{\lambda},B_{1})
is a two-sided Brownian motion with drift
\lambda
, independent of the two-sided Brownian motion
W_{0}^{\lambda}=\overleftarrow{R}(Y_{0}^{\lambda},B_{1})
. Hence,

(W_{0}^{\lambda},Y_{1}^{\lambda},B_{2},\ldots,B_{n})\qquad\text{are mutually independent}.

Inductively, assume that
Y_{r}^{\lambda}
is a two-sided Brownian motion with drift
\lambda
and

(W_{0}^{\lambda},\ldots,W_{r-1}^{\lambda},Y_{r}^{\lambda},B_{r+1},\ldots,B_{n})\qquad\text{are mutually independent}.

Apply Theorem
C.2
to
Y_{r+1}^{\lambda}=\overleftarrow{D}(Y_{r}^{\lambda},B_{r+1})
and
W_{r}^{\lambda}=\overleftarrow{R}(Y_{r}^{\lambda},B_{r+1})
to continue the induction. Part (i) is proved.
The proof of Part (ii) is the proof of Theorem 3.3 in
[
SV10
]
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
2.3
is achieved through the coupling of
q_{r}^{\lambda},Y_{r}^{\lambda}
, and
W_{r}^{\lambda}
. The following theorem is the key.

Theorem C.4

(Zero-temperature analogue of Theorem 3.1 in
[
ARAS20
]
)
.

Fix real numbers
t>s
,
\lambda>0
, and
\gamma>\lambda^{-2}>\delta>0
. Then, with probability one,

(C.2)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl[\,L_{(0,t),(n,n\delta)}(\mathbf{W}^{\lambda})-L_{(0,s),(n,n\delta)}(\mathbf{W}^{\lambda})\,\bigr]\leq B_{0}(s,t)-\lambda(t-s)=-Y_{0}^{\lambda}(s,t)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl[\,L_{(0,t),(n,n\gamma)}(\mathbf{W}^{\lambda})-L_{(0,s),(n,n\gamma)}(\mathbf{W}^{\lambda})\,\bigr],

and

(C.3)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl[\,L_{(0,t),(n,n\delta)}(\mathbf{W}^{\lambda})-L_{(1,t),(n,n\delta)}(\mathbf{W}^{\lambda})\,\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq q_{1}^{\lambda}(t)\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl[\,L_{(0,t),(n,n\gamma)}(\mathbf{W}^{\lambda})-L_{(1,t),(n,n\gamma)}(\mathbf{W}^{\lambda})\,\bigr].

Here,
L_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{W}^{\lambda})
is the last passage time between points
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}
when
\mathbf{W}^{\lambda}:=\{W_{m}^{\lambda}\}_{m\geq 0}
is the random environment. (
W_{m}^{\lambda}
need not be defined for
m<0
since all points
(m,t)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
in the above expression satisfy
m\geq 0
.) While Theorem
C.4
is not explicitly stated in
[
ARAS20
]
, all necessary lemmas are provided in Section 4 of that paper.

Lemma C.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be any countable set. Consider the space
\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{A}}
of functions
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, equipped with the Borel product
\sigma
-algebra. Fix
\theta>0
. Then,

\displaystyle\bigl\{h_{0}^{\theta}(t):t\in\mathcal{A}\bigr\}\overset{d}{=}\bigl\{-B_{0}(t)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}t:t\in\mathcal{A}\bigr\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigl\{v_{1}^{\theta}(t):t\in\mathcal{A}\bigr\}\overset{d}{=}\bigl\{q_{1}^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}}(t):t\in\mathcal{A}\bigr\}.

Proof.

This proof is a multivariate extension of the “Proof of Theorem 2.5, assuming Theorem 3.1” on page 1937 of
[
ARAS20
]
. This method originated in the setting of the log-gamma polymer in
[
GRASY15
]
. From definition (
2.6
) of
h_{0}^{\theta}
,
h_{0}^{\theta}(0)=0=B_{0}(0)
. Hence, to prove the first statement, it is sufficient to show that

\bigl\{h_{0}^{\theta}(s,t):s,t\in\mathcal{\mathcal{A}},s<t\bigr\}\overset{d}{=}\bigl\{-B_{0}(s,t)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}(t-s):s,t\in\mathcal{\mathcal{A}},s<t\bigr\}.

Theorem
2.3
establishes that, for fixed
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
,

h_{0}^{\theta}(s,t)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl[L_{(0,s),(n,n\theta)}(\mathbf{B})-L_{(0,t),(n,n\theta)}(\mathbf{B})\bigr]\text{ a.s.}

By Theorem
C.3
,
\{W_{m}^{\lambda}\}_{m\geq 0}
is a field of independent, two-sided Brownian motions. By equality of distribution between
\{W_{m}^{\lambda}\}_{m\geq 0}
and
\{B_{m}\}_{m\geq 0}
, the first inequality of (
C.2
) implies that if
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\}
and
\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\}
are finite subsets of
\mathcal{A}
with
s_{i}<t_{i}
for all
i
, and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}
, then whenever
\theta<\lambda^{-2}
, or equivalently,
\lambda<\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(-h_{0}^{\theta}(s_{i},t_{i})\geq x_{i},\;\;1\leq i\leq k\bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl(B_{0}(s_{i},t_{i})-\lambda(t_{i}-s_{i})\geq x_{i},\;\;1\leq i\leq k\bigr).

Since
\lambda>0
and
s_{i}<t_{i}
for all
i
, the right-hand side
decreases as
\lambda
increases, so taking
\lambda\nearrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}
gives us

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(-h_{0}^{\theta}(s_{i},t_{i})\geq x_{i},\;\;1\leq i\leq k\bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(B_{0}(s_{i},t_{i})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}(t_{i}-s_{i})\geq x_{i},\;\;1\leq i\leq k\Bigr).

The inequality on the right of (
C.2
) with
\lambda\searrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}
establishes the reverse inequality.

The same argument works for
v_{1}^{\theta}
, utilizing this monotonicity for
\gamma<\delta
:

\displaystyle q_{1}^{\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,B_{1})(t)=\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}\{B_{1}(s,t)+B_{0}(s,t)-\gamma(t-s)\}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}\{B_{1}(s,t)+B_{0}(s,t)-\delta(t-s)\}\geq q_{1}^{\delta}(t).\qed

Appendix D
Time reversal

Theorem D.1
.

Let
Z,B,Y,C
be continuous functions satisfying
Z(0)=B(0)=Y(0)=C(0)=0
and

\lim_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}(B(t)-Z(t))=\lim_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}(C(t)-Y(t))=\mp\infty.

Then,

Y=D(Z,B)\text{ and }C=R(Z,B)\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad Z=\overleftarrow{D}(Y,C)\text{ and }B=\overleftarrow{R}(Y,C),

The equalities above denote equality as functions of
t
. If either of the above equivalent conditions are satisfied, then also
Q(Z,B)=\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)
.

The following lemmas will help to prove Theorem
D.1
.

Lemma D.2
.

Let
Z,B:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be continuous functions satisfying
X(0)=B(0)=0
and

\lim_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}(B(t)-X(t))=\mp\infty.

For a function
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, recall that we define
\widetilde{f}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by
\widetilde{f}(t)=-f(-t)
.
Then, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

Q(Z,B)(-t)=\overleftarrow{Q}(\widetilde{Z},\widetilde{B})(t),\qquad-D(Z,B)(-t)=\overleftarrow{D}(\widetilde{Z},\widetilde{B})(t),\qquad\text{and}\qquad-R(Z,B)(-t)=\overleftarrow{R}(\widetilde{Z},\widetilde{B})(t).

Proof.

This is a routine check, using the definitions.
∎

The following is a well-known fact, but is often stated without proof, so we include full justification for the sake of completeness. For example, it appears as Equation (1.4) in
[
Pit75
]
and Equation (13) in
[
OY01
]
.

Lemma D.3
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function such that

\lim_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}f(t)=\pm\infty.

Set
F(t)=\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}f(s)
. Then,

(D.1)

\inf_{t\leq s<\infty}(2F(s)-f(s))=F(t).

Proof.

The left-hand side of (
D.1
) is

\inf_{t\leq s<\infty}(2F(s)-f(s))=\inf_{t\leq s<\infty}\bigl(2\sup_{-\infty<u\leq s}f(u)-f(s)\bigr).

For all
s\geq t
,
\sup_{\infty<u\leq s}f(u)
is greater than or equal to both
f(s)
and
\sup_{-\infty<u\leq t}f(u)
. Therefore,

2\sup_{-\infty<u\leq s}f(u)-f(s)\geq\sup_{-\infty<u\leq t}f(u)+f(s)-f(s)=F(t).

This establishes one direction of Equation (
D.1
). To show the other direction, we show that there exists
s\geq t
such that

2F(s)-f(s)=F(t).

Note that
f(t)\leq F(t)
and that
\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}f(s)=\infty
by assumption. Hence, by continuity of
f
,
f(s)=F(t)
for some
s\geq t
. Let

s^{*}=\inf\{s\geq t:f(s)=F(t)\}.

Then,
F(s^{*})=F(t)
. Therefore,

2F(s^{\star})-f(s^{*})=2F(t)-F(t)=F(t).\qed

Proof of Theorem
D.1
.

Assume that
Z,B,Y,C
satisfy the conditions of the Theorem. First, assume that
Z=\overleftarrow{D}(Y,C)
and
B=\overleftarrow{R}(Y,C)
.
By definitions (
3.6
)–(
3.8
),

\displaystyle Z(s)

\displaystyle=Y(s)+\sup_{-\infty<u\leq s}\{C(u,s)-Y(u,s)\}-\sup_{-\infty<u\leq 0}\{C(u,0)-Y(u,0)\}

\displaystyle=C(s)+\sup_{-\infty<u\leq s}\{Y(u)-C(u)\}-\sup_{-\infty<u\leq 0}\{Y(u)-C(u)\},\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle B(s)

\displaystyle=C(s)+\sup_{-\infty<u\leq 0}\{C(u,0)-Y(u,0)\}-\sup_{-\infty<u\leq s}\{C(u,s)-Y(u,s)\}

\displaystyle=Y(s)+\sup_{-\infty<u\leq 0}\{Y(u)-C(u)\}-\sup_{-\infty<u\leq s}\{Y(u)-C(u)\}.

Then,

\displaystyle D(Z,B)(t)=Z(t)+Q(Z,B)(0)-Q(Z,B)(t)

\displaystyle=Z(t)+\sup_{0\leq s<\infty}\{B(0,s)-Z(0,s)\}-\sup_{t\leq s<\infty}\{B(t,s)-Z(t,s)\}

\displaystyle=B(t)+\sup_{0\leq s<\infty}\{B(s)-Z(s)\}-\sup_{t\leq s<\infty}\{B(s)-Z(s)\}

\displaystyle=Y(t)+\sup_{-\infty<s\leq 0}\{Y(s)-C(s)\}-\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}\{Y(s)-C(s)\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sup_{0\leq s<\infty}\bigl\{Y(s)-C(s)-2\sup_{-\infty<u\leq s}\{Y(u)-C(u)\}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sup_{t\leq s<\infty}\bigl\{Y(s)-C(s)-2\sup_{-\infty<u\leq s}\{Y(u)-C(u)\}\bigr\}.

To show that this equals
Y(t)
, it is therefore sufficient to show that for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}\{Y(s)-C(s)\}=\inf_{t\leq s<\infty}\bigl\{2\sup_{-\infty<u\leq s}\{Y(u)-C(u)\}-(Y(s)-C(s))\bigr\},

which follows from Lemma
D.3
. The proof that
R(Z,B)(t)=C(t)
follows by the same reasoning. The converse then follows by the previous case and Lemma
D.2
: if
Y=D(Z,B)
and
C=R(Z,B)
,
\widetilde{Y}=\overleftarrow{D}(\widetilde{Z},\widetilde{B})
and
\widetilde{C}=\overleftarrow{R}(\widetilde{Z},\widetilde{B})
, so
\widetilde{Z}=D(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{C})
and
\widetilde{B}=R(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{C})
. Hence
Z=\overleftarrow{D}(Y,C)
and
B=\overleftarrow{R}(Y,C)
.

We finish by showing that
Q(Z,B)=\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)
whenever the two equivalent conditions of the theorem are met. For all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle Y(t)

\displaystyle=D(Z,B)(t)=Z(t)+Q(Z,B)(0)-Q(Z,B)(t),\text{ and }

\displaystyle Z(t)

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{D}(Y,C)=Y(t)+\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(t)-\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(0).

Putting these two equations together,

Q(Z,B)(t)-\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(t)=Q(Z,B)(0)-\overleftarrow{Q}(Z,B)(0).

This is true for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, so
Q(Z,B)(t)-\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(t)
must be equal to some constant. Recall that

Q(Z,B)(t)=\sup_{t\leq s<\infty}\{B(t,s)-Z(t,s)\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)(t)=\sup_{-\infty<s\leq t}\{C(s,t)-Y(s,t)\}.

By the limit conditions of the theorem, maximizers exist for each of the supremums above, so
Q(Z,B)
and
\overleftarrow{Q}(Y,C)
both achieve a minimum value of
0
. Hence, the constant must be
0
.
∎
